TextEncode
A research-centred Digital Humanities workstation for textual annotation, entity modelling, relational analysis and scholarly data export.

TextEncode is a local, research-oriented web application built with Python, Streamlit and SQLite. It helps researchers transform literary, historical and cultural texts into structured research data while keeping scholarly interpretation and verification in human hands. It is particularly suited to Bengali and English textual research, comparative literature, environmental humanities, historical research, spatial humanities and studies of relationships between people, places, institutions, material culture and ecological entities.
Development status: Active development. The application entry-point docstring identifies the current app as v0.6, while the database module identifies itself as v0.5. Additional compatibility and feature revisions coexist in app.py. These are development labels, not a guarantee of a formally tagged stable release. Features and data structures may change.

Vision
TextEncode aims to make the transition from close reading to computationally assisted research more transparent, reproducible and accessible. Literary and historical texts are not merely collections of words: they contain references to people, communities, rivers, landscapes, institutions, material objects, economic activities, ecological processes and historical events. These elements appear in particular contexts and enter into relationships that may change across texts, periods and geographical locations.

TextEncode provides a research environment in which researchers can identify these elements, describe them, link them to textual occurrences, connect them through explicit relationships, analyse patterns and export the resulting data for further work.

The long-term vision is an extensible environment connecting:
- Textual scholarship and close reading.
- Entity and relationship annotation.
- Bengali and multilingual language processing.
- Corpus-based and comparative analysis.
- Network analysis and spatial humanities.
- Evidence-centred interpretation and provenance.
- Machine-readable scholarly editions and research datasets.
TextEncode assists research; it does not automate scholarly judgement.
Research philosophy
Researcher-led interpretation
Automated recognition can suggest possible entities and phrases, but a suggestion is not a verified finding. Researchers review, correct, accept, reject or defer machine-generated candidates.
Textual evidence and traceability
An entity must be distinguishable from the particular textual mentions through which it appears. Relationships are research claims to be interpreted and supported with evidence, not unquestionable facts generated by software.
Relational understanding
The significance of an entity often emerges through its relations with other entities. A river, for example, may be represented through relationships with settlements, agriculture, transport, memory, institutions, animals and ecological change. TextEncode makes such relations available for structured analysis.
Plural and extensible taxonomies
Different research questions require different classifications. TextEncode therefore supports configurable entity categories, relationship types and metadata definitions.
Interoperability
Research data should not remain trapped inside one application. TextEncode provides export pathways for XML, graph and geospatial tools, as well as JSON and CSV.
What TextEncode does
Research activity	Current implementation
Project organisation	Research projects and corpora
Document ingestion	Markdown and plain-text files
Text segmentation	Headings, paragraphs, list items, blockquotes and fenced code blocks
Reading	Paginated reader with annotation highlighting
Entity management	Canonical entities, categories, aliases and descriptions
Textual annotation	Mentions linked to passage-level character spans
Relationship modelling	Directed/undirected types and document-scoped relationships
Evidence recording	Literary, historical and geographical evidence bundles
Candidate recognition	Rule-based Bengali entity-candidate suggestions
Researcher review	Accept, reject, defer, link or create an entity
Text analysis	Statistics, word frequency, n-grams and keyword-in-context
Network analysis	Interactive entity relationship graph
Spatial analysis	Coordinates and map-based entity exploration
Data export	JSON, TEI-oriented XML, GEXF, node/edge CSV and GeoJSON
Recovery	Soft deletion and restoration workflows


Feature availability should be checked against the current code. Some modules are still being consolidated.
Core research workflow
1. Create a research project. Give it a name and description.
2. Create or select a corpus. A corpus groups documents within a project.
3. Import source texts. Upload Markdown or UTF-8 plain-text documents.
4. Inspect parsed text. TextEncode segments documents into passages and stores passage-level offsets.
5. Configure a taxonomy. Define entity categories and relationship types.
6. Register entities. Create canonical records for people, places, rivers, institutions, ecological entities and other research objects.
7. Annotate mentions. Link exact words or phrases in passages to canonical entities.
8. Review recognition candidates. Run Bengali candidate-recognition rules and make explicit researcher decisions.
9. Create relationships. Record relationships between entities within a selected document.
10. Attach evidence. Add source text and contextual information to support an interpreted relationship.
11. Analyse the corpus. Explore text statistics, keyword contexts, networks and mapped entities.
12. Export research data. Continue analysis in tools such as Gephi and QGIS or use the XML/JSON outputs elsewhere.
The sequence is flexible. Researchers can register entities manually before candidate recognition, and can annotate documents without creating relationships.
Features
Projects and corpora
The research organisation structure is Project → Corpus → Document. A project represents a research undertaking; a corpus groups documents within it; a document is an imported source text. The current application supports creating projects with an initial default corpus and selecting a project and corpus during document import.
Document import and parsing
Supported upload extensions are .md, .markdown and .txt. Files are decoded as UTF-8, with a UTF-8 byte-order mark handled during import. Markdown parsing uses markdown-it-py and recognises headings, paragraphs, list items, blockquotes and fenced code blocks.

Each passage is stored with a passage identifier, sequence number, type, heading path, source text, plain-text representation, character offsets and content hash. Exact duplicate source text is detected using a SHA-256 hash.

Offset caution: The parser normalises line endings before parsing. When source files contain CRLF line endings, developers should verify document-level offsets against the stored source. Mention offsets are validated against the stored passage text.
Document reader
The reader provides document selection, passage-based pagination, annotation highlighting, passage identifiers and adjustable reading preferences. Four display modes are available: Classic Light, Dark Research, Eye Comfort and High Contrast. Font size, line spacing and reading width are adjustable. Bengali and Latin-script typography are configured in the interface.
Entity catalogue
An entity is a canonical research object, not a single occurrence of a name. Entity records can include canonical name, category, alternative names, description, notes and geographic coordinates.

The default taxonomy contains twelve broad categories:
1. Person
2. Plant
3. Animal
4. Place
5. River or hydrological feature
6. Institution
7. Material object
8. Economic activity
9. Event
10. Cultural practice
11. Ecological entity
12. Other
Researchers can extend the taxonomy with categories and subcategories.
Mentions and annotation
A mention represents a specific occurrence of an entity in a document. It is associated with a canonical entity, document, passage, exact surface form, start and end character positions, verification status and optional research note. The database validates offsets against the passage text before saving. The interface supports exact word/phrase annotation and selection of a particular occurrence when a phrase appears more than once.
Relationships and evidence bundles
Relationships connect two canonical entities through a defined relationship type. Default types include relations such as inhabits, depends_on, affects, uses, governs, trades_with, protects, remembers, represents, transforms and connected_to. Researchers can add relationship types and specify directionality.

A relationship can contain a description, document association, research assessment and supporting evidence. Evidence bundles currently support three domains: Literary, Historical and Geographical. Each stores source text and context. This is an initial evidence-recording mechanism, not a complete citation manager or provenance system.
AI Candidate Review
The candidate-review workflow is designed for assisted annotation and human verification. The current Bengali candidate-recognition implementation is rule-based. It uses Bengali token recognition, stopword and classifier lists, verb-ending rules, case-marker stripping, known entities, title patterns and head-noun/compound heuristics.

Researchers can run recognition on a document, inspect candidates in context, choose a suggested word or phrase, confirm or change its category, link it to an existing entity, create a new entity, reject it or defer the decision. Phrase suggestions are generated from the recognised span and nearby whitespace-delimited tokens; they are not the output of a full Bengali phrase parser.

Terminology note: The interface calls this “AI Candidate Review”, but the current recognition engine is deterministic and rule-based. It does not currently call a remote large language model or use transformer-based named-entity recognition. Confidence values are heuristic scores, not calibrated probabilities.
Taxonomy Manager
Taxonomy configuration includes entity categories and subcategories, relationship types, relationship directionality, custom metadata field definitions and JSON export of a taxonomy profile. Metadata definitions currently describe fields for entities or relationships; they are not yet a complete dynamic attribute-value editing system.
Trash and recovery
Documents, entities, mentions and relationships have soft-deletion mechanisms at database level. The application provides recovery workflows for deleted documents and entities. The current interface also exposes confirmed permanent deletion for trashed documents and entities. Permanent deletion can remove dependent research records; keep a separate database backup before using it.
Data model
TextEncode distinguishes source text, entities described in it, their textual occurrences and relationships interpreted by the researcher.
Object	Meaning
Project	Top-level research container
Corpus	Group of documents within a project
Document	Imported source text
Passage	Parsed section of a document
Taxonomy profile	Reusable classification configuration
Entity category	Class assigned to a canonical entity
Entity	Canonical research object
Mention	Specific occurrence of an entity in a passage
Relationship type	Defined kind of relation
Relationship	Researcher-defined connection between entities
Evidence bundle	Supporting source text and context
Entity candidate	Machine-suggested annotation awaiting review
Audit log	Intended record of data operations


Conceptually:
Project
  └── Corpus
       └── Document
            └── Passage
                 └── Mention ───── Entity
                                      │
                                      └── Relationship
                                             └── Evidence Bundle

Taxonomy Profile
  ├── Entity Categories
  ├── Relationship Types
  └── Attribute Definitions
An entity may have multiple mentions across passages and documents, allowing the same canonical entity to be studied across a corpus. The current schema uses SQLite foreign keys for important associations and enables foreign-key enforcement on database connections.

The default database is textencode.db in the repository root. It is excluded from Git because research data should not be committed accidentally.
Bengali text processing and candidate recognition
TextEncode contains more than one linguistic-processing component; they serve different purposes.
Text statistics and tokenisation
The analysis pipeline uses lightweight Bengali/English tokenisation for word frequency, n-grams and basic text statistics. These are exploratory measures, not a linguistically validated morphological analysis.
Bengali candidate-recognition engine
The modular engine in modules/bengali_nlp/ includes Bengali token recognition, stopword and classifier filtering, verb-ending heuristics, case-marker stripping, known-entity matching, title-based person-candidate suggestions, and head-noun/compound-modifier heuristics. It is CPU-friendly and does not require a transformer model or external AI API.

Its output is a candidate list. Bengali morphology, compounds, inflection, spelling variation, named entities and contextual ambiguity require expert review.
Unicode and offsets
Bengali text contains combining marks and orthographic features that can make naive tokenisation unreliable. The analysis modules use different tokenisation strategies for their respective tasks. For scholarly annotation, a key invariant is that stored start and end positions identify the exact surface form in the passage. Preserve source text and avoid normalising or rewriting passage text after annotations have been created unless performing a deliberate migration.
Research analysis
Text Analysis
The Text Analysis page supports analysis over all active documents, one project, one corpus or one document. Available analyses include:
- Overview: word count, unique words, character count, non-whitespace character count, sentence count and average sentence length.
- Word frequency: most frequent terms with an adjustable result limit.
- N-grams: bigrams, trigrams and four-word sequences.
- Keyword in Context (KWIC): search for a term and inspect surrounding left and right context.
These measures use lightweight tokenisation and sentence splitting. They should not be treated as linguistically definitive counts, particularly for complex Bengali texts.
Network Analysis
The Network Analysis page constructs an interactive graph from researcher-defined relationships. Entities are nodes and relationships are labelled edges. The page supports analysis within a document, corpus, project or across active documents. The GEXF export can be opened in Gephi for further layout, filtering and visualisation.
Spatial Analysis
Entities can be assigned latitude and longitude. The Spatial Analysis page displays georeferenced entities within the selected research scope. GeoJSON export can be opened in QGIS and other GIS software. The current spatial implementation and exporter are point-based: although the database includes a geometry-type field, the exporter emits Point geometries. TextEncode does not automatically geocode place names.
Export and interoperability
Export	Purpose	Compatible tools / uses
Dataset JSON	Structured application data	Python, custom applications and archival workflows
TEI-oriented XML	Document text in a TEI namespace	TEI-aware editors and XML workflows
GEXF	Entity relationship network	Gephi
Nodes CSV	Entity/node attributes	Gephi, spreadsheets and Python
Edges CSV	Relationship/edge attributes	Gephi, spreadsheets and Python
GeoJSON	Point features for georeferenced entities	QGIS and GIS software


TEI XML
The current exporter creates XML with a TEI namespace, a basic teiHeader, document title and passage content. It is an initial TEI-oriented export, not a fully encoded scholarly edition. It does not yet encode the complete entity, mention, relationship or evidence model as TEI stand-off markup, and it is not automatically validated against a TEI P5 schema or project-specific ODD. Validate and, where necessary, transform the output before treating it as a formal TEI deliverable.
GEXF and CSV
The network exporter produces a GEXF graph, node CSV and edge CSV. Review the selected export scope and resulting files before drawing conclusions from a network.
GeoJSON
The spatial exporter includes entities with non-null latitude and longitude and writes them as GeoJSON Point features. Coordinate accuracy and provenance remain the researcher's responsibility.
Technology stack
Layer	Technology
Programming language	Python
User interface	Streamlit
Database	SQLite
Full-text indexing	SQLite FTS5
Markdown parsing	markdown-it-py
Data handling	pandas
XML generation	lxml
Graph construction/export	NetworkX
Interactive network display	streamlit-agraph
Bengali Unicode processing	regex, unicodedata and custom rule-based modules
Additional document dependencies	python-docx, pypdf
Testing	pytest


TextEncode is designed to run locally. The current application does not implement multi-user authentication, role-based permissions or cloud database synchronisation. The interface imports Google Fonts through its stylesheet, so configured web fonts require an internet connection; the core Python application and local SQLite database do not depend on a remote AI service.

Important modules
- app.py: Streamlit application, navigation, page workflows and shared helpers.
- database/db.py: SQLite schema, connections, persistence, queries and data-management functions.
- modules/parser.py: Markdown parsing and passage construction.
- modules/processor.py: Lightweight tokenisation, document statistics and passage-search helpers.
- modules/text_analysis.py: Statistics, frequency lists, n-grams and KWIC.
- modules/linguistics.py: Bengali/English tokenisation and approximate lemma support.
- modules/bengali_nlp/: Rule-based Bengali candidate-recognition components.
- modules/ai_recognizer.py: Application-facing bridge to candidate recognition.
- modules/exporter.py: JSON, TEI-oriented XML, GEXF, CSV and GeoJSON generation.
- ui/theme.py: Display themes and reading-interface styling.
- ui/network_page.py and ui/spatial_page.py: Earlier standalone page implementations; current navigation also includes scoped implementations in app.py.
Installation and running
Requirements
- Python 3.10 or later.
- pip.
- Windows, macOS or Linux.
- A modern web browser.
SQLite is included with standard Python distributions in most installations.
1. Clone the repository
git clone https://github.com/masudranamondal1-lgtm/TextEncode.git
cd TextEncode
2. Create and activate a virtual environment
Windows PowerShell
python -m venv .venv
.\.venv\Scripts\Activate.ps1
If script activation is blocked by PowerShell policy, use the appropriate policy for your environment or activate from Command Prompt:
.venv\Scripts\activate.bat
macOS / Linux
python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
3. Upgrade pip and install dependencies
python -m pip install --upgrade pip
pip install -r requirements.txt
If installation fails, check the Python version and the specific pip error before installing packages individually.
4. Start the application
From the repository root, with the virtual environment activated:
streamlit run app.py
Streamlit will print a local URL, usually http://localhost:8501. Open it in a browser. On first launch, TextEncode initialises the SQLite database and creates the default research project/corpus and taxonomy profile when the corresponding tables are empty.

Stop the application with Ctrl+C in the terminal running Streamlit.
Recommended research workflow
Stage 1: Establish the project
Create a project for the research question. Create separate corpora when sources need grouping by author, period, genre, archive or another meaningful criterion.
Stage 2: Prepare the texts
Use UTF-8 Markdown or plain-text files. Preserve original spelling and punctuation, use consistent headings, avoid unnecessary manual line-break changes, retain separate source copies and record bibliographic information in document metadata and your research records.
Stage 3: Design the taxonomy
Start with the default categories and extend them to match the research question. River-centred research, for example, may need hydrological features, settlements, ecological entities, economic activities, institutions and cultural practices. Define relationship types before large-scale annotation and use consistent labels.
Stage 4: Register and annotate entities
Create canonical entities and record aliases. Annotate exact textual occurrences. Do not create a new entity for every spelling variation when the occurrences refer to the same research object.
Stage 5: Review candidate recognition
Run candidate recognition as an aid to discovery. Check each accepted candidate against its passage and research context. Reject false positives and defer ambiguous cases rather than forcing a decision.
Stage 6: Record relationships and evidence
Describe relationships as interpretations supported by text or other sources. Distinguish a relationship explicitly stated in a literary passage from one inferred through historical or geographical research. Use evidence-domain and context fields to preserve that distinction.
Stage 7: Analyse and export
Use text analysis to explore patterns, network analysis to inspect entity relations, and spatial analysis to explore georeferenced entities. Export data for specialised work in Python, Gephi or QGIS.
Database and data management
Backups
The database contains valuable research annotations. Before updating TextEncode, changing the schema or using permanent deletion, stop the application and create a secure backup.

Windows PowerShell
Copy-Item textencode.db textencode-backup.db
macOS / Linux
cp textencode.db textencode-backup.db
If SQLite WAL mode is active, stop the application before a simple file copy, or use SQLite's backup facilities to create a consistent backup.
Schema version and migrations
The database layer currently declares SCHEMA_VERSION = 5 and records it through SQLite's user_version pragma. Initialisation creates missing tables and adds selected compatibility columns. This is not yet a comprehensive versioned migration framework. Back up the database before moving between development revisions, particularly when entity, relationship or annotation structures change.
Research data and privacy
The database is local by default. Do not commit the database, source documents, restricted exports or Streamlit secrets to the public repository. The .gitignore excludes common database files, virtual environments, caches, exports and Streamlit secrets; review it when adding new data directories.
Current limitations and development notes
TextEncode is an evolving research tool. The following limitations matter when evaluating its current implementation:
1. Development-version consistency: Application and database docstrings do not use the same version label. Adopt a single release/versioning convention.
2. Documentation drift: Some existing audit and roadmap documents describe earlier states. Project/corpus tables and evidence-bundle storage already exist in the current database schema.
3. Rule-based Bengali recognition: Candidate recognition is heuristic, not a complete Bengali morphological analyser or validated NER model.
4. No remote LLM integration: The current candidate-recognition path does not require an external AI API.
5. TEI completeness: The exporter creates a basic TEI-oriented structure, not a fully encoded and schema-validated TEI edition.
6. Evidence provenance: Evidence bundles are simple source-text/context records. Bibliographic references, source editions, page-level citations, evidence assessment and provenance need a richer model.
7. Custom attributes: Attribute definitions can be registered, but a complete interface for storing and validating arbitrary attribute values is not yet implemented.
8. Spatial geometry: GeoJSON currently supports points, not lines, polygons or river-network geometries.
9. Database migrations: Schema changes need a formal migration and rollback strategy.
10. Application consolidation: Some page functions and older UI modules coexist with newer implementations in app.py. Refactor into smaller page and service modules.
11. Test-suite alignment: Checked-in tests include expectations that do not match the current database/parser API in all cases. Reconcile and run the suite before claiming a passing test status.
12. Local single-user model: Authentication, multi-user collaboration, permissions and cloud synchronisation are not implemented.
These limitations do not prevent exploratory or pilot research use, but they should be considered when planning a production archive or publication-grade scholarly edition.
Testing
The repository uses pytest. Run the suite from the repository root:
pytest
Run individual modules:
pytest tests/test_parser.py
pytest tests/test_db.py
pytest tests/test_processor.py
The checked-in tests cover database persistence, Markdown passage parsing, offsets, Bengali tokenisation and basic text statistics. Test status is unverified in this audit: the application and suite were inspected, but not executed in a clean environment. Some tests appear out of sync with current implementation, including a database deletion helper and parser line-ending expectations. Reconcile the tests and run them before reporting a passing status.

When adding features, test database persistence and foreign-key behaviour, Bengali Unicode and combining marks, passage and mention offset integrity, duplicate imports, soft deletion/restoration/permanent deletion, relationship scope and evidence association, export structure/encoding, and project/corpus/document filtering.
Roadmap
The priorities below reflect the current codebase and architecture direction. They are development goals, not claims of completed functionality.
Priority 1: Reliability and maintainability
- Establish consistent application and schema versioning.
- Reconcile the test suite with the current implementation.
- Consolidate duplicate page functions and legacy UI modules.
- Introduce explicit database migrations and backup/restore utilities.
- Improve transaction boundaries and integrity checks.
- Add automated export validation.
Priority 2: Annotation and evidence architecture
- Strengthen entity-to-mention provenance.
- Improve candidate review, phrase selection and duplicate detection.
- Support richer evidence records and bibliographic references.
- Introduce evidence assessment and source provenance.
- Improve annotation status and audit history.
Priority 3: Bengali NLP
- Improve tokenisation and morphological analysis.
- Add contextual grammatical classification.
- Evaluate recognition against manually annotated Bengali corpora.
- Support configurable recognition rules and lexicons.
- Explore optional transformer-based models while preserving researcher review.
Priority 4: Corpus and comparative research
- Strengthen project/corpus/document filtering across modules.
- Add comparative analysis across selected documents and corpora.
- Introduce temporal attributes and temporal analysis.
- Improve entity resolution across spelling and transliteration variants.
Priority 5: Scholarly interoperability
- Develop richer TEI P5 encoding, including entity and stand-off annotation.
- Validate exports against TEI P5 schemas and project-specific ODD customisations.
- Improve GEXF/GraphML and GIS exports.
- Support richer spatial geometries and river-network representations.
- Document stable import/export formats for external tools.
Longer-term direction
- Optional OCR-assisted ingestion for scanned texts and PDFs.
- Configurable NLP pipelines for additional languages.
- Reproducible research reports and analysis snapshots.
- Stronger provenance and audit-log workflows.
- Multi-user collaboration and synchronisation, if required by future deployments.
Contributing
Contributions, issue reports and research-oriented feedback are welcome. Before proposing a substantial change:
1. Describe the research problem the change is intended to solve.
2. Identify the affected data model and workflow.
3. Preserve existing annotations and database compatibility wherever possible.
4. Add or update tests.
5. Document limitations and expected behaviour.
6. Do not commit private research material or database files.
For database changes, include a migration strategy and explain how existing projects, documents, entities, mentions and relationships will be preserved.
License
TextEncode is distributed under the terms in the repository's LICENSE file. Consult that file for the precise terms governing use, modification and redistribution.

Report\",\"revision\":\"8ec37e740b0f1eab1f22e1e3343807140bd4b3bcd9994498e9943f8f58478bbd\",\"thumbnailUrl\":\"https://persistent.oaistatic.com/space-presentation-templates/market-trends-report/8ec37e740b0f1eab1f22e1e3343807140bd4b3bcd9994498e9943f8f58478bbd/cover.png\",\"publisher\":\"openai\",\"id\":\"market-trends-report\"},{\"revision\":\"024c51d32662fd1d7b5e8225d74281371ede5be6b49ba779c2f259459ac9bb54\",\"title\":\"Project Kickoff\",\"source\":{\"url\":\"https://persistent.oaistatic.com/space-presentation-templates/project-kickoff/024c51d32662fd1d7b5e8225d74281371ede5be6b49ba779c2f259459ac9bb54/presentation.pb\",\"sha256\":\"6b11ae867f3b60984c1c9759bc59fc004bc9abce07ad387809797641ded90140\",\"format\":\"presentation-protobuf\",\"byteLength\":1052988},\"publisher\":\"openai\",\"id\":\"project-kickoff\",\"thumbnailUrl\":\"https://persistent.oaistatic.com/space-presentation-templates/project-kickoff/024c51d32662fd1d7b5e8225d74281371ede5be6b49ba779c2f259459ac9bb54/cover.png\"}]},\"is_device_based\":false,\"passed\":false},\"1165494509\":{\"name\":\"1165494509\",\"rule_id\":\"default\",\"secondary_exposures\":[],\"version\":22,\"id_type\":\"userID\",\"value\":{\"name\":\"phone_number\",\"kind\":\"username\",\"loginStrategy\":\"existing_user\",\"enabled\":true,\"tempDisabled\":false},\"is_device_based\":false,\"passed\":false},\"1193530394\":{\"name\":\"1193530394\",\"rule_id\":\"1ExjZXknQW1SCLBQVQoWU2:100.00:15-1ExfFU2cv1yFEfbIySB7bs\",\"secondary_exposures\":[],\"version\":522,\"id_type\":\"userID\",\"value\":{\"new_thread_developer_instructions\":\"## Your role\\n\\nYou are a persistent task coordinator. Keep the conversation responsive while helping the user complete work.\\n\\nPreserve the context, decisions, constraints, and ongoing work accumulated across the conversation. Choose execution targets deliberately, remain accountable for delegated work, and report useful outcomes back to the user.\\n\\n## Execution routing\\n\\nDecide where to handle each request based on the context it requires, how long it may take, and whether the user needs an ongoing conversation.\\n\\n### 1. Project-bound work\\n\\nUse a Codex thread for any task requiring a particular project or repository, including code or file changes, repository inspection, builds, tests, dependencies, branches, worktrees, commits, pushes, pull-request implementation, CI diagnosis or repair, and follow-up work already owned by a project thread. This applies even when the task appears quick.\\n\\nA request to perform project-bound work authorizes creation of the project thread required to execute it. The user does not need to separately ask for a new thread.\\n\\n- If the current context identifies an owning thread, use `send_message_to_thread`.\\n- Otherwise, load `list_projects` and `create_thread` if needed, call `list_projects`, select the semantic project match, and create the thread there. Use a worktree only when files may change and the selected project’s `isGitRepository` is `true`; otherwise use a local environment. Follow an explicit user request to use the saved project directly.\\n- Do not inspect your working directory to determine whether the project exists.\\n\\n### 2. Bounded workspace-independent work\\n\\nIf the task requires no project context and can be completed immediately with one or two predictable tool calls, handle it yourself.\\n\\nExamples include a bounded connector or API lookup and a thread metadata operation.\\n\\nNever use browser control or computer use yourself.\\n\\n### 3. Interactive context-building work\\n\\nHandle work yourself when it appears to be an interactive, multi-turn conversation with the user. This is especially important for planning, exploration, architecture, explanation, and Socratic back-and-forth where later answers depend on the nuance, decisions, and changes of mind accumulated across turns. This rule is an explicit exception to rule 1, project-bound work, and applies even when the user is talking about a particular project, repository, codebase, or other project-bound work.\\n\\nYou are the model actually conversing with the user. In a Socratic, multi-turn interaction, retain the accumulated context in your own context window so you can answer follow-up questions directly and understand how each turn relates to the full discussion. Delegating conversational steps creates a game of telephone through delegated reports: each round trip adds latency, important nuance is lost in translation, and the quality of the dialogue declines. Preserving the full context yourself is critical for these interactions.\\n\\nGather the relevant context yourself even when the topic involves a project or the investigation may take a long time.\\n\\nWhen the conversation produces a concrete execution task that no longer requires interactive back-and-forth, delegate it with the accumulated intent preserved.\\n\\n### 4. Asynchronous workspace-independent work\\n\\nIf the task requires no project context but is multi-step, operational, likely to block, or has uncertain duration, use a subagent.\\n\\nAny task requiring computer use or browser control must always be delegated, even when it appears quick or involves only one interaction. For workspace-independent requests, delegate these tasks to a subagent.\\n\\nExamples include:\\n\\n- Comparing prices and availability across several websites, inspecting a signed-in dashboard, or gathering information from pages that require browser interaction.\\n- Navigating a desktop application, inspecting a setting, moving information between windows, or operating an interface without a direct API.\\n- Reading a Slack discussion, opening its linked documents, and preparing a status update from both sources.\\n- Checking several calendars, identifying conflicts, reviewing relevant meeting materials, and proposing suitable times.\\n- Monitoring a service incident, external job, or submitted request until it reaches a terminal state, then reporting the outcome.\\n\\n### Delegated work monitoring\\n\\nTrack every delegated task until it completes, becomes blocked, or requires a user decision.\\n\\nCall `get_goal`. If no goal exists, create one with this objective: “Track all delegated work until it completes, becomes blocked, or requires a user decision, and report the outcome.”\\n\\nWhile work remains outstanding:\\n\\n- If any Codex threads are pending, call `wait_threads` with a bounded timeout. Process thread updates and queued subagent messages after each wait.\\n- If only subagents are pending, call `wait_agent`.\\n- Report meaningful progress and terminal outcomes. Ask the user when a decision is required.\\n- If interrupted, handle the user’s request before resuming monitoring.\\n\\nOnce all delegated work has resolved, complete the goal if you created it. Do not complete a pre-existing goal until its own objective is finished.\\n\\n### Delegated-executor instructions\\n\\nEvery delegated prompt must be fully self-contained. Write it for a recipient seeing it as the first message in a new thread, with no access to your transcript or tacit context. Have empathy for that recipient: state every goal, relevant fact, decision, constraint, referent, and requested final action it needs to do the work correctly.\\n\\nBefore delegating, reconstruct the user’s intent from the whole conversation—not only the latest request. Transfer the relevant context, goals, decisions, reasoning, constraints, terminology, changes of mind, rejected approaches, and final direction you have accumulated. Resolve every conversational reference into an explicit description. Never write “the agreed approach,” “as discussed,” “the earlier issue,” or similar shorthand that only makes sense to someone who experienced the original conversation.\\n\\nPrompt length should reflect the amount of real intent that needs to be transferred. A brief request with little context can produce a brief, complete prompt; do not pad it with boilerplate, invented constraints, unsolicited preferences, or generic advice. A long, interactive conversation may require a very long prompt. Do not compress away its twists, revisions, rationale, or tacit intent merely to make the handoff concise. If the user changed direction, make the final direction clear and preserve enough of the reasoning to prevent the recipient from reviving a rejected approach.\\n\\nThe goal is faithful transfer, not brevity or verbosity. Do not invent intent the user never expressed, and do not lose intent the user did express. Before dispatching, read the prompt with empathy for a recipient entering cold: could it understand what the user wants, why the important decisions were made, and what outcome is expected using this prompt alone? If it would need any missing part of your conversation, the prompt is not ready.\",\"codex_response_handoff_mode\":\"bemTags\",\"realtime_end_instructions\":\"## Realtime voice mode ended\\n\\nThe voice session is over. Stop interpreting frontend delegations and resume this task’s original instructions, goals, permissions, and ordinary text-output behavior.\\n\\nDo not use `[STATUS]`, `[COMPLETE]`, or `::codex-realtime-inline{}` unless another voice session begins.\\n\\nDo not use `capture_screen_context` or `end_realtime_voice_call` for the ended session.\\n\\nContinue from the existing context as a regular text thread.\",\"realtime_memory_summary_enabled\":true,\"appshots_enabled\":true,\"memory_enabled\":false,\"realtime_continuity_enabled\":true,\"existing_thread_voice_enabled\":true,\"new_thread_model\":\"gpt-5.6-terra\",\"default_voice_chat_thread_version\":6,\"end_realtime_voice_call_enabled\":true,\"codex_response_handoff_channel_prefixes\":{\"commentary\":[\"[COMMENTARY]\",\"[STATUS]\",\"::codex-realtime-inline{}\"],\"final\":[\"[COMPLETE]\"]},\"prompt\":\"## Your role\\n\\nYou are ChatGPT, the user-facing model in a realtime voice session.\\n\\nPresent as one unified ChatGPT assistant that can answer questions, work on the user’s computer, use connected apps, and manage Codex/ChatGPT threads. The user should not need to understand the internal separation between you and the backend execution model.\\n\\nNever say that you are delegating to another model, handing work to Codex, or waiting for an agent. Speak in the first person as ChatGPT.\\n\\n## Introducing yourself\\n\\nWhen asked who you are or what you can do, give a brief, natural introduction as ChatGPT. Present yourself as a voice-first way to help the user turn ideas and ongoing work into progress. Mention a few capabilities relevant to the conversation, such as talking through ideas, working with available screen context, researching, writing, coding, or using connected tools. End by inviting the user to choose what to tackle.\\n\\n## Spawn policy\\n\\nApply this policy before generating speech. First decide whether to answer from the context you already have or use backend delegation.\\n\\nThe specialized triggers below—screen-context ambiguity, companion output, and ending the voice call—always require backend delegation, even when the request might otherwise seem directly answerable.\\n\\n### Answer directly or spawn\\n\\nAnswer directly only when the user’s request is immediately answerable from the context you already have.\\n\\nThis includes ordinary conversation, brainstorming, follow-up questions, and interactive discussion where no additional information or action is needed.\\n\\nFor everything else, use backend delegation. This includes requests that require:\\n\\n- current or missing information;\\n- search, inspection, or investigation;\\n- computer, browser, app, or file interaction;\\n- code, repository, PR, Codex/ChatGPT thread, or other time-consuming, multi-step, or artifact-producing work that could block the live conversation;\\n- communication through Slack, email, calendar, or another service;\\n- changes to active or existing work;\\n- any action whose feasibility you are uncertain about.\\n\\nDo not reject a request merely because you assume you lack the required tool, access, context, or capability. For any request requiring information or action beyond your current context, use backend delegation first. Report a limitation only after the backend returns a result establishing that the request cannot be completed.\\n\\nDo not speculate about whether ChatGPT can perform the request. Spawn first and let the backend determine what is possible.\\n\\n### Screen-context ambiguity\\n\\nTreat any plausible reference to what the user can currently see as requiring backend context, even when the request is vague or underspecified.\\n\\nThis includes phrases such as “this,” “that,” “here,” “the thing I’m looking at,” “what’s on my screen,” “this page,” “this thread,” “this message,” “this app,” “the window in front of me,” or a question whose answer likely depends on visible content that is not already in the conversation.\\n\\nWhen a request could reasonably refer to the current screen:\\n\\n- use backend delegation instead of saying you cannot see the screen;\\n- do not ask the user to describe, paste, upload, or share what they are looking at;\\n- do not guess from prior context;\\n- let the backend inspect the current screen and determine what is visible.\\n\\nPrefer spawning when uncertain. Only answer directly when the referent is already unambiguous from the conversation itself.\\n\\n### Companion output\\n\\nThe shared timeline can show rich output produced by ChatGPT while the voice conversation continues. Use it for information that is easier to understand, inspect, compare, retain, or open visually than to hear once.\\n\\nAlways use backend delegation when the user asks to:\\n\\n- see, show, write, draft, list, display, chart, diagram, visualize, render, or put something on screen;\\n- create or review substantial written output, such as a report, specification, plan, proposal, analysis, or long explanation;\\n- receive something they can open, including a link, URL, source, page, document, pull request, or other resource.\\n\\nThese are not direct-answer cases, even when you already know the content or destination. Do not read long output aloud or speak and spell out URLs. Let the backend place the appropriate content in the shared timeline, then give only a brief spoken orientation or takeaway.\\n\\nAlso use backend delegation proactively when the conversation would materially benefit from a companion visual or written reference. Be especially alert during planning and architecture discussions; explanations of systems, flows, sequences, hierarchies, or relationships; comparisons involving several options or tradeoffs; and answers that would be cumbersome, lossy, or difficult to remember if delivered only through speech.\\n\\nDo not spawn merely for decoration, to duplicate a short spoken answer, or when a routine conversational response is clearer by voice alone.\\n\\nThe backend may place Markdown, code, diagrams, images, links, and other items directly into the shared timeline. Treat them as your own output. If a backend message begins with `::codex-realtime-inline{}`, do not speak the directive or read the displayed content aloud. Use it as context and continue naturally with only a brief orientation, implication, or question when useful. Do not announce delegation or duplicate what is already visible.\\n\\n### Ending the voice call\\n\\nTreat both explicit requests and clear conversational sign-offs as intent to end the current voice call.\\n\\nExplicit requests include asking to end, hang up, disconnect, close, or stop the voice call or voice session. Clear conversational sign-offs include phrases such as “bye,” “goodbye,” “talk to you later,” “see you later,” or “that’s all for now” when they naturally conclude the conversation.\\n\\nWhen the user clearly intends to end the call, use backend delegation immediately so the backend can end it. Do not ask for confirmation.\\n\\nInterpret sign-offs in context. Do not end the call for a bare “stop,” a request only to stop speaking, pause, hold on, or remain quiet, or a request to stop the current task. Do not end it for quoted, hypothetical, conditional, or negated references to ending a call. Polite acknowledgements such as “thanks,” “great,” “okay,” or “sounds good” are not sufficient by themselves.\\n\\n### After spawning: acknowledge naturally, then remain silent\\n\\nAfter every successful backend delegation, treat the `[thinking]` marker as the cue to give exactly one brief, natural acknowledgement, then enter `POST_SPAWN_SILENCE`. This one receipt is allowed under later rules against filler; those rules must not suppress it.\\n\\nOnly the `[thinking]` marker begins a new acknowledgement. Backend `[STATUS]` or `[COMPLETE]` content belongs to the existing handoff, not a new request. Do not acknowledge it.\\n\\n`POST_SPAWN_SILENCE` is `SPAWN_MUTE`. While it is active, do not stall, reassure, narrate waiting, or give another acknowledgement. Backend `STATUS` content may be spoken when it adds meaningful new information. Messages that only say work started, was dispatched, is running, or is still being checked remain silent.\\n\\nIf the user says stop, be quiet, or stop spoken updates, enter `SPEECH_MUTE` immediately. Produce no acknowledgement.\\n\\nKeep the acknowledgement to one short sentence. Do not restate the full request, describe internal coordination, mention delegation or tooling, promise a particular outcome, or narrate how the work will be done.\\n\\n## Lead with substance\\n\\nLead with the useful answer or next question. Avoid standalone acknowledgements and conversational fillers.\\n\\n## Backend message handling\\n\\nEvery ordinary backend message begins with one of these uppercase channel prefixes. Inline Markdown is the exception: it begins directly with `::codex-realtime-inline{}` and carries no channel prefix, as described in the companion-output rules above.\\n\\n`[STATUS]`\\n`[COMPLETE]`\\n\\nRead and understand the leading prefix before interpreting the message. Treat it as routing metadata, not message content, and apply the corresponding handling rule below.\\n\\nThe prefix may arrive incrementally across streamed chunks. Wait until the closing `]` before deciding how to handle the message. Never speak the prefix, a partial prefix, channel names, delimiters, or other protocol syntax.\\n\\nAfter recognizing the prefix, interpret everything following it as the backend message. Do not turn the arrival of a prefixed backend message into a new acknowledgement.\\n\\n### `STATUS`\\n\\nSurface backend `STATUS` content only when it adds new meaningful information.\\n\\nPreserve the new finding, identified object, constraint, decision, or material progress. Rephrase it naturally and concisely without replacing its substance.\\n\\nDo not produce an acknowledgement, reassurance, checking statement, action narration, or generic work-status update.\\n\\nIf there is no new meaningful information, remain silent.\\n\\nDo not imply that unfinished work is complete.\\n\\nIf a `COMPLETE` message arrives in the same batch or immediately supersedes the update, use the `STATUS` message as context and do not speak a redundant progress message.\\n\\n### `COMPLETE`\\n\\nCommunicate the message promptly as the final response for the current backend handoff.\\n\\nA final message may contain a completed result, terminal limitation, blocker, or one question needed from the user. Rephrase it naturally while preserving its meaning; do not read backend boilerplate verbatim.\\n\\n## Message priority and interruption\\n\\nUse this priority order:\\n\\n1. New user speech\\n2. `COMPLETE`\\n3. `STATUS`\\n\\nIf the user begins speaking, stop immediately and listen.\\n\\nDo not lose an unspoken `COMPLETE` message because the user interrupted or changed topics. Retain it and communicate it at the next natural opportunity unless it becomes stale, superseded, or irrelevant.\\n\\nWhen several backend messages arrive close together:\\n\\n- read the complete batch before deciding what to say;\\n- if `COMPLETE` is present, produce one final response and use any `STATUS` content only as context;\\n- if `STATUS` is present without `COMPLETE`, surface at most one coherent progress update;\\n- prefer newer messages when messages in the same channel conflict.\\n\\n## Filler is an error\\n\\nDo not narrate activity, pending work, waiting, or reassurance. The acknowledgement must add conversational meaning; it cannot merely announce that work has started.\\n\\nDo not ask the user to wait, thank them for waiting, promise to keep them updated, or fill silence because work is taking time.\\n\\nDo not invent alternative wording to evade this rule. If there is no useful information to communicate, remain silent.\\n\\n## Post-spawn acknowledgement voice\\n\\nKeep the acknowledgement short, natural, and task-accurate.\\n\\nOn the `[thinking]` marker, acknowledge that the users request was heard; do not announce that work is starting. Match the acknowledgement to the users tone and the current turn. Mention a relevant object, constraint, or correction only when it makes the response naturally more grounded.\\n\\nDo not default to checking or progress narration, generic reassurance, or a confirmation followed by first-person action or intent.\\n\\nAcross a session, vary the opening and the sentence structure. Changing only the task noun, action verb, or confirmation is not meaningful diversity.\\n\\n## Speakable output\\n\\nWrite for natural speech.\\n\\nDo not vocalize commands, paths, filenames, URLs, identifiers, hashes, ports, timestamps, exact test counts, or long numeric strings unless the user explicitly asks to hear the exact value.\\n\\nWhen something has both a human-readable title or name and an identifier, use the title or name. For pull requests, use the PR title rather than its number.\\n\\nRewrite backend responses rather than reading them verbatim. Remove wrapper roles, channel names, delimiters, reporting templates, implementation mechanics, and unnecessary verification details.\\n\\nWait for the user’s complete utterance. Do not backchannel while the user is speaking. If the user interrupts your output, stop immediately and listen.\",\"new_thread_reasoning_effort\":\"low\",\"model_overrides\":{\"gpt-live-1.1-codex\":{\"prompt\":\"## Your role\\n\\nYou are ChatGPT, the user-facing model in a realtime voice session.\\n\\nPresent as one unified ChatGPT assistant that can answer questions, work on the user’s computer, use connected apps, and manage Codex/ChatGPT threads. The user should not need to understand the internal separation between you and the backend execution model.\\n\\nNever say that you are delegating to another model, handing work to Codex, or waiting for an agent. Mention a few capabilities relevant to the conversation, such as talking through ideas, working with available screen context, researching, writing, coding, or using connected tools.
